ParlaMint-NO_2018-12-05.ana.ud557e70	Honourable co-representatives! Former depositor Terje Knudsen passed away on 20 November 2017, 75 years old. Terje Knudsen was in the period 1997 to 2001 a major representative of the Progress Party and was a member of the SSC of Storting. He was also head of the Progress Party Group in Hordaland County from 1991 to 1997 and Vice-Chairman of the Hordaland Progress Party from 1994 to 1996. Terje Knudsen had a varied professional career, worked for both the shipping industry, industry and various sales activities as well as his political work. Before Terje Knudsen became active in the Progress Party, he was an active municipal politician for the Bergen Right. Terje Knudsen also took on a number of public functions and was active in a number of boards and selections. Terje Knudsen was, through his work at many levels, an important co-worker for the Progress Party, until he became an independent representative from March 2001. A long life of political activity is now over. We bring peace to Terje Knudsen's memory.

ParlaMint-NO_2018-12-05.ana.ud557e190	I want to start by thanking the Committee for a good cooperation in an incredibly complicated and comprehensive case. The issue we are dealing with today concerns issues of important social security, which are intended to ensure that police and defence can secure citizens in a threat situation, and that important functions such as water supply, electricity, telecommunications and other important functions can be secured, if necessary, by security forces. The case has a long history. Following the terrorist incidents on 22 July 2011, an overall increase in police and defence cooperation was expected. Instructions on the protection and protection of objects through the use of defence and police forces in peace, crisis and war, the so-called object protection directive, were one of the measures to achieve it. The Special Committee, led by Knut Arild Hareide, was clearly in expectation that the time limits for securing were to be met in line with the requirements of the Safety Act and the Regulation on Object Safety. In the examination of the Riksrevisions Document 1 for 2016-2017 and Appendix No. 1 to Document 1 for 2016-2017, it became clear that neither the basic protection under the Object Safety Regulation nor the hedged forces under the Object Protection Instructions were in line with the Storting assumptions. At the time of the examination of these documents — and an open and closed consultation in this regard — the members of the government were given the impression that the situation was better than the report at the time. The treatment at that time ended with very strong criticism of the government's lack of follow-up to Stortingen's assumptions and the very special fact that the Stortinget unanimously asked the Riksrevision for a new report, to clarify the status at the time of treatment. Despite strong criticism, the Prime Minister did not take part in the debate in Stortinget on the matter. It is the new report, the Riksrevision's study on the follow-up of object safety, Document 3:11 for 2017-2018, which is the basis for the setting we now have for treatment. As part of the discussion, the Committee has asked a number of questions to the Minister of Defence, the Minister of Justice and the Prime Minister. We have held an open and closed consultation with current and former state councils, current trade unions and Prime Minister. The report shows that the government's presentation of the previous case was a makeup of reality. The report we are now considering, and have worked on in the committee, has shown that there is still a lot to be done before securing important objects in society is what the Stortinge has foreseen. About the police, the Riksrevision says that the basic protection of protective objects is still inadequate. During the Committee's work, it has been published by the Prime Minister that only two of the twelve shielded objects are virtually secured in line with the regulations. Nor did the Stortinge’s assumption that the Ministry’s proposals for the State budget must reflect the need to ensure protective features were followed up by specific funds in the 2018 budget. That is why the Riksrevision says: ‘The Court of Auditors takes a very serious view that the basic protection of protective features is not a priority of the Ministry of Justice and Defence.’ ‘Very serious’ is the strongest criticism that can be used by the National Audit Office and refers to circumstances where the consequences for society or the citizens concerned are very serious, such as: the risk to life and health. In the case of the defence, the Riksrevision continues: ‘The ground protection of the displayable objects is still deficient.’ The defence does not have ‘a comprehensive overview of the condition’ at the end of 2017. [[...]] So at the end of 2017, the defense does not have a complete overview of the state. They go on to say, ‘Deficient basic protection of the shielding features of the Defence may lead to the loss of important functions in the event of adverse events.’ [[...]] And... ‘The Court of Auditors takes a very serious view that the Ministry of Defence has not taken the necessary steps to improve the basic protection of the shielding features of the Defence.’ The National Audit Office points out that the Ministry of Justice and Defence and the Ministry of Defence have improved the follow-up to police and defence use of security forces. But there is still much to be done before the decisions and assumptions of the Storting have been followed up. That is the point the members of the government and the representatives of the government parties most often point to in this report, as an example of much work being done. But then it's part of the story that also at that point the Riksrevision uses the expression "very serious" about today's status. A theme that has re-examined in the previous report, in this report and in the hearings is the lack of common understanding of the object protection directive. In spite of the fact that the Chief of Defense, the Head of Police Directorate and the Prime Minister claim that there is no disagreement between the Defence and the police about the understanding of the instructions, the Minister of Justice said in his letter to the Riksrevision: ‘I see, like the National Audit Office, the need to review and, where appropriate, revise the instructions with a view, inter alia, to clarify responsibilities and the understanding and cooperation between the two sectors.’ Only now has the work started, although the previous report showed clearly that this was one of the key problems. Also with regard to the defence process with the designation of civil key objects, it is pointed out that it has been poorly followed up. Lack of adequate safeguards may have implications for preventive State security. The National Audit Office further points out that there is still a lack of clarification and analysis of the capacity of the police and Home Security authorities to secure and protect objects using security forces. Neither the Ministry of Justice nor the Police Directorate has an overview showing police capabilities to secure and protect objects. When we see the report from the National Audit Office, which we are now dealing with, it becomes clear that last year the members of the government drew a makeup picture of the actual situation. The government did not inform about the expected cost of securing the police's own objects. It has not notified the Stortinget that the police's objects will only be secured once in the 2020s. It has not notified that the defence will have secured its objects only in 2025, 10 years after the deadline, nor notified of the expected costs of the security measures. Instead of taking all the documented deficiencies seriously, the government’s propaganda apparatus has deployed all the resources to try to misplace and de-dramatise the content of the report. First of all, Minister of Justice and Defence Tor Mikkel Wara, who, in the news of the day on 7 June, just after the report was presented, stated that the report concerned only the own construction of the police and the defence. It was even more serious that Prime Minister Erna Solberg, in a televised party leader debate from Arendal in August, said: ‘No, it’s not about water and electricity. It is about the police's own buildings and the defence's own buildings.» [[...]] And she said, ‘This is one thing: the physical buildings of the police and the defence.’ These claims are directly incorrect. They show that the Prime Minister does not take matters sufficiently seriously. In the 2013 election campaign, Erna Solberg said: ‘That’s why I have always thought that good politicians can’t concentrate on being offensive in the media, they have to follow up their decisions, dry off engaging in the details and how the policy actually works for ordinary people. [[...]] This is where I believe the Stoltenberg government has failed the most. There has been too much media management and too little implementation. That is why I believe that the Prime Minister and the Prime Minister's office must take responsibility for their implementation." It is in stark contrast to the fact that Prime Minister Solberg, who is also the head of the Government's security commission, admits that she did not know how bad object protection was before the Riksrevision presented its first report. It is also inappropriate that, in the previous consultation, she pointed out that a misreport by Minister of Justice Anundsen in the 2016 budget is not capable of distinguishing between reporting on the situation following the instructions and following the regulation — even referring to the entire justice sector, which is far more than the police’s own objects. Hardly randomly, the Prime Minister chose to lay the foundation of the seriously delayed response centre in Taraldrud a few days before open consultation in the committee. There she revealed that the police were responsible for the basic security of 12 objects, four of them would get their solution when the emergency centre was put into service. At the same time, it was revealed that only two out of 12 objects have – with some exceptions – satisfactory protection. It was with great surprise the committee registered that the prime minister thus referred to the information we knew from the non-public parts of the report, when it took care of her media management. It was therefore natural for an overall committee to ask for an overview of which parts of the non-public part of the report could be released. As a result, there is now a report with a great deal of gossip. But I can say so much as that what is still outside of publicity, more detailedly substantiates the basis of the Riksrevision's strong criticism in the open part of the report. One of the things that emerges clearly is that when Chief HV, Eirik Kristoffersen, in the hearing, claims that more HV areas than in 2014 are more ready to respond by the end of 2017, it is wrong – without me being able to refer to the actual figures. Neither the Minister of Defence nor the Prime Minister has so far corrected this misinformation in the hearing, but they will have the opportunity later today. In large parts of the setting, there is consensus. The seriousness is underlined by the strong criticism of the government, which is also promoted by the government parties. In fact, a large part of the critical comments in this setting is a unanimous committee behind it. Therefore, I hope that after this treatment – if the government continues to sit – the setting will lead the government to take responsibility for security more seriously, that the Prime Minister will follow up matters below, and that we will have a comprehensive and comprehensive review of plans and expected progress and costs in securing critical objects for society, as a unanimous committee requires in the vote. It is very serious when the government does not follow up the Storting's assumption of important social security, in the light of the Constitutional Law in Section 82: ‘The Government shall communicate to the Storting all the information necessary for the handling of the matters it submits. No member of the Government shall provide incorrect or misleading information to the Storing or its organs.’;» In this respect, it is very serious and highly critical, in the majority’s view, that the Stortingen has not been properly informed by the government on the situation, on expected costs and on progress. In the light of the Committee's view, it is necessary to present the following proposals for decisions, which are also set out: ‘The government’s regrets the lack of follow-up of object protection with regard to the basic protection of protective features and the protection and protection of objects by the use of security forces from the police and the Defence of Home Affairs, including the lack of information to the Government on deferrals and costs.’
ParlaMint-NO_2018-12-05.ana.ud557e279	The National Audit Board’s follow-up report on the government’s work on object security has been widely discussed in the Committee. It has been a demanding job. Firstly, this latest report deals with a very short period of time. Everyone in this room knows that comprehensive action cannot be taken without going through thorough investigations and that economic arrangements must be made through the milestones underlying the work of the Stortinge. It takes longer than the report deals with. It has therefore been impossible to process the report without looking at what has been the basis of object protection work, starting almost ten years ago until today, and looking at how work started in 2017 has led to results and follow-up after the end of the report. Secondly, it has been difficult to get an overview and an understanding of the changes that are constantly taking place, affecting both the choice of solutions and the need for security by the police and the defence to be implemented. Both changes in the external threat picture and the development of new technical solutions make entirely different solutions today than was the case just a few years ago. Thirdly, I have to say that it has been difficult to relate to such a large material. Very demanding is also the material raised after a whole of 44 questions from the Committee or members of the Committee to the Government, with comprehensive and clear answers from the Government. However, it deserves a comment that the comprehensive question and the equally comprehensive answers do not appear in any way to have contributed to or reflected in the comments written in the case. It's for thought. The two hearings of the Committee on the matter provided further insight into the challenges facing the government and the work done to secure central features. When the government of Stoltenberg laid down the safety regulations for its work with effect from 1 January 2011, it was clear that there was in no way today’s need for protection underpinning neither deadlines nor economic priorities. It was established that the new provisions themselves would not entail significantly increased costs and would be implemented within three years. In retrospect, there is no doubt that the assumptions were unrealistic. This was also confirmed by the Chief of Defence Haakon Bruun-Hanssen, who said at the open hearing in Stortinget on 27 August this year: ‘The competence and resource need to map and describe the large number of displayable objects has been far more demanding than initially assumed in 2011. The need for resources to implement measures is also far more extensive and time consuming than initially foreseen. This is, in my opinion, the main reason why the time limit laid down in the regulation has not been complied with.» At the same hearing, the Director of the National Security Authority, Kjetil Nilsen, had a similar conclusion in response to why the protection of vulnerable objects in the justice and defence sectors is not in place: ‘One reason for this is that the resource needs of the implementation of the Feature Security Code under the Safety Act in 2011 were assessed too low. In retrospect, there has been a greater need for resources both in terms of economics and in terms of available competences.’» It is clear that on several occasions it would have been natural to take a foot in the ground and to make assumptions and deadlines realistic. It was already clear in 2012/2013 that neither the financial assumptions nor the deadlines could be met. It would then have been natural to put in place realistic assumptions for the work. After the government change in 2013 and the extension of the one-year period, it was also not realistic to complete the hedge as foreseen. I understand that communication to the Stortinget is demanding in cases where several departments have different, independent tasks, while these are part of a whole. It requires someone to take overall responsibility for the overall communication. Once the government in budgetary propositions has notified its bets and dispositions, this has been important feedback, but it has not been suitable to give the Storting an overall insight into the status of securing display objects. That is why I also understand that there is criticism of the government for a lack of information on new plans and objectives for implementing the measures. I am therefore very pleased that, particularly the Prime Minister, but also several of the Councils, at the hearing in August was so clear in the recognition that this should have been notified back to Stortinget in another way. The Prime Minister said this: ‘Knowledge of the complexity and demanding object protection work has been a gradual recognition. I see that this is something my government should communicate earlier and more clearly and more collectively to Stortinget, and I take it for myself – on behalf of the government. So we should have given more general information about this." It's a confession and feedback it says respect for. The Prime Minister's follow-up with concrete promise to get to Stortinget with further answers to updated plans should also give the Stortinget confirmation of the government's willingness to complete the work. Her statements show that she takes this matter very seriously, and this provides a good basis for the government's further work. The main outcome of the National Audit Office’s reports and debates here in Stortingen must be that the work on object protection is intensified and completed through concrete plans and the allocation of resources. It's good to see the work on object protection going forward. Still more objects are secured. Other features have been given temporary solutions pending more comprehensive and time-consuming final solutions. Further plans for securing and new solutions for the remaining objects we know today are being systematically worked on. However, it is important to bear in mind that objects and security needs are constantly changing, and that there will always be some new objects under investigation or work going on. This is a natural part of the continuous effort to ensure critical infrastructure. It's a fact that completed object protection has been, and is, a moving goal. Both in the 2017 budget and in their 2019 budget proposal, the government has shown in concrete terms that they are following the offensive plans they have announced. Police ICT security has been a priority, and efforts to do so through the last year’s budgets have resulted in substantial changes in infrastructure and data centres being secured under new safety laws. The large investments that provide the basis for the police to have modern and forward-looking training facilities and locations secured in line with the requirements of the new Security Act have received the necessary funding. Plans to ensure that all screening features in the police are properly secured during 2020 are on track. Also in the defence budget is the work on object protection provided resources that enable the work to be carried out. The defense has a very large number of single points to ensure, but it is also important to remember that some of these objects are located in areas that have an external security and good security. This means that the more vulnerable points can be prioritised in the first place. Home security is better rusted than in a very long time. Through several non-social budgetary settlements, HV has been given the means to re-establish a long-term lag in maintenance, spare parts and ammunition, and HV is now given the means to systematically carry out good practice. I am surprised to note that the opposition does not seem to have confidence in either the head of defence or HV and states that the answer, chief of HV, gave in the hearing, does not correspond to their own perception of the actual situation, and therefore is wrong. It is very special that members of the Stortinget rely on better detailed knowledge and assessment of this than top qualified leaders of the Defence. For our part, we have no reason to doubt Eirik Johan Kristoffersen's summation of the filling up of the Home Guard Emergency Reserves, nor his conclusion that: ‘The operational status of Home Protection has been continuously improving in recent years.’ Although the number of policemen is not directly related to object protection, this is also part of the total response effort of this government. Since the government joined, the police staff has increased by approx. 2 600 annual works. Of this, approx. 1 500 years of police work. The target of two police officers per 1000 inhabitants by 2020 is on track. It contributes to increased security for the population. So to a couple of points in the National Audit Office's report that, in my opinion, doesn't give a properly leftover impression. In the report, the Riksrevision criticises the Police Directorate and the Defence for the lack of a common understanding of the object protection directive. This is clearly rejected from both political and professional leadership. After a thorough check in their organisations, they can conclude that this is not the case. There has been a discussion about the timing of a review of the instructions, and the parties have agreed to start this work when new law and the National Audit Office's report are in place, but the report adds a negative to the parties here that is not real. Also, with regard to the criticism of temporary compensatory measures for objects waiting for a new location, there is a strange observation. The National Audit Office points out that there are no concrete plans for compensatory measures pending final basic insurance. Of course, this is true, as compensatory measures have been established. If they had fully satisfied the requirements of the Security Act, they would not have been temporary compensatory measures. It is claimed that the government has not prioritised object protection. It's obviously wrong. What has been done on object protection has actually happened to Erna Solberg as Prime Minister. Over tough years of refugee crisis, oil price falls and high unemployment, however, the government has cut over 7 billion. kr on the civil side and approximately 10 brd. to the defence sector for enhanced preparedness and object protection. This is a priority! So it's the opposition's right to think it's too small. That criticism would be a lot more credible if they themselves had made completely different priorities in this area, which I can't see is the case. In particular, when the red-green government resigned in 2013, two months before the expiry of the deadline in the regulation, there was no indication that it had prioritised this work. Of course, there is no excuse for the sitting government, but when it always refers to the original deadlines, it must be appropriate to point out that this was a demanding starting point. Finally, I must say that I am surprised that so much of the political environment seems to be more concerned with a political game around the matter than quickly to put in place the necessary protection of objects, increased preparedness and security so that we can defend ourselves in the event of crises or threats. It is good for the government to take that responsibility and now clearly answer this to Stortinget.
ParlaMint-NO_2018-12-05.ana.ud557e340	The national audit studies are important, especially in areas where public authorities are to provide safety and security to citizens. In its report, the National Audit Board has made strong criticisms of the follow-up to object security. This is a criticism to be taken seriously. And taking this seriously is about delivering – providing security and security to the inhabitants. Let me therefore make it clear immediately that the Group of the Advance Party has full confidence that today's government is delivering on this important mission. At the same time, we are pleased that the Prime Minister has been clear that progress in this demanding work should have been continuously communicated to Stortinget, so that the complexity and costs of this work – and that the timetable proposed would not have been kept – had come to light. Let me take a brief look: the Special Committee, set up after the 22nd of July, provided that all objects should be classified by the end of 2012, and that the implementation of security measures should take place as soon as possible and no later than three years after the entry into force of the Object Safety Regulation, i.e. during 2013. The Committee stressed the importance of its follow-up within the specified time limits. We know in retrospect that neither the classification nor the implementation of object protection measures was carried out by the previous government as foreseen. This should have been in place two months after the government's resignation in 2013. In other words, everything that has been done in this area has been done in practice by the current government. In addition, the time-limits and costs involved in the implementation of the Object Safety Regulation were unrealistic. We see that very clearly in retrospect. The Regulation entered into force on 1 January 2011. In Ot.prp. No 21 for 2007-2008, which was presented by the Red-Green Government, we can read on page 42 that it was considered that ‘the proposals taken together should not involve significantly increased costs’. In kilograms. Res. from October 2010, where the regulation entered into force, it states that ‘the new provisions therefore do not entail new costs in themselves’. In retrospect, object protection has cost billions of dollars, and it will cost billions of dollars in the future to be invested. Therefore, the criticism that the Riksrevision targets in its document affects as much the previous government as it does at present when looking at what was done in advance and subsequently at adopted law and regulation, and what clear recommendations were there from the special committee. So what has today's government done in this area? The current government has cancelled over 7 billion. kr for civil protection measures and approx. 10 B.C.E. kr to the defence sector. Examples of this are new police helicopters, a police emergency room for $2.6 billion. kr, the joint counter-terrorist centre, the joint cyber coordination centre and the replacement of the defence radio line network with the fibre network. Only to replace the radio network is it set at 900 million. kr. Ørland Air Station has received increased appropriations of 1.6 billion. kr for protection and protection. In its position and comments, the Committee stressed that the Grand Duchy was the granting authority and also recalled that there was a broad consensus on the appropriations for these emergency measures. There are unanimous remarks that have been made today in this area. The opposition has shown that there are still large gaps, but has not even come up with a single penny in increased appropriations that have been earmarked for the purpose of the report and during the period covered by the report. Nevertheless, the opposition chooses to promote distrust of the government because it has not given enough money. Let me remind you again – as you say in a unanimous remark – that it is the Stortinge which is granting authority. It may also be worth remembering that there was no distrust of the then government of Stoltenberg after the 22nd of July terror, even though the Gjørv Commission, which then presented its report, made very clear criticism of the preparedness and lack of follow-up, among other things. in the case of the closure of Grubbegata in the Government Quarter and several other measures not implemented. The criticism that then emerged from the Commission's report is probably the strongest criticism that is directed at some government ever. Nevertheless, the Storting concluded that the work to ensure the safety and security of the citizens was a work that we would jointly deliver, and it was concluded that there should be no distrust of the sitting government at that time. Today, when we take stock of the situation, we can see that all the measures proposed by the Gjørv Commission and the Special Committee are either implemented or implemented. Many of the measures are of a continuous nature. In this area, one will never completely reach the goal. The goal is moving all the time, and new measures must be taken continuously as the threat picture changes in the world. The list of measures following the terrorist attacks on 22 July is available in the Notice. St. 10 for 2016–2017, Risk in a Secure Society. The list is based on these recommendations – 31 recommendations of the Commission and 14 decisions of the Specific Committee. It is then very exciting to read through and see that it is or will be delivered on this. Let me address several important aspects of this work that the government is delivering on. Home Security has a nationwide territorial responsibility, where the protection of the key points of Defence is the design of the Home Security strength structure. The structure shall also facilitate allies' reception, ensure the delivery of their own and other countries' forces and provide adequate situational understanding. Both the head of defence and the head of HV have been aware that the Home Office has sufficient capacity to secure all the key points. The National Audit Service assumes that Home Protection should be able to secure and protect all civil and military key objects, while also ensuring all objects that the police have pre-planned with the assistance of the Defence. This has never been the premise or the design of the structure of Homeland Protection. If such a situation should arise in Norway, then we will be at war, and then the total resources of the defence must be prioritised and used where they give the greatest fighting power and fighting ability. Furthermore, it should be noted that if civil objects are protected by military personnel in a war situation, these objects become a legitimate military objective. Only civil objects critical of their defence capability will be protected. Home security has been significantly strengthened in recent years through budgetary conciliation between the four non-socialist parties. Only in 2017 the Heimevertet was strengthened by 191 million. kr, e.g. to re-establish a long-standing aftermath on maintenance, spare parts and ammunition. It is of direct concern that the head of defence and the head of HV are not believed by the majority of the Committee when they say that the operational status of Home Security has improved in recent years with the introduction of new material and more time for practice. Then to the emergency centre at Taraldrud: After a long-standing localisation debate on a new emergency centre, the government decided in August 2012 that the emergency centre would be established at Alnabru. Project Alna, which started with a letter of assignment to Statsbyggen in February 2013, was completed when the draft project was in place in December 2014. The background was that the site at Alnabru was too small for the resources planned to be colocated there, that is, the police helicopter service, the emergency team, and the dog service in Oslo police district. One of the alternatives that existed when Alnabru was elected was the Lahaugmoen military camp, which was to be destroyed. This camp had many of the facilities needed — for example: 200 metres of firing range – and has a good location between the centre of Oslo and the Gardermoen. After Alnabru was elected, Lahaugmoen was sold to the private sector and thus not available as an alternative. In December 2017, the Stortinget adopted the establishment of the National Emergency Response Centre at Taraldrud, Ski Municipality, with a cost framework of 2.6 billion. kr and with a planned move by the end of 2020. The emergency centre shall address the need for improved training facilities and the necessary facilities for the emergency team, the bombing service, the crisis and hostage handlers and the helicopter service. When the opposition criticises the government for the fact that permanent basic protection is not in place on all the twelve shielding objects in the field of justice, they are largely co-responsible that this is not in place. The Emergency Response Centre will secure one third of the police protectionable objects. Had e.g. Lahaugmoen was elected instead of Alnabru by the Stoltenberg government, the emergency centre had probably been in place several years ago. In addition, it is very striking that the same batches that are now criticising the government for not delivering have tried to stop – or at least postpone – construction on Taraldrud, which would have further delayed the process considerably. We know this through proposals that the opposition has promoted, and the debate that has been held in the Committee on Justice. So to Ørland Air Station: When Ørland was selected as the main fighter base with Evenes as a forward-looking operating base in 2012, Ørland was planned to be built as a peace base. As a consequence of the change in the security policy image — with an increased need for protection and security — costs have increased by 1.6 billion. kr to secure this base alone. This was a little bit of an insight into the work the government has done in this area. Should you go through everything that has been done by action, probably the debate would have been extended by many hours. The government must have a good conscience for everything that has been delivered together with supporting parties in the Storting on the budgets for which it has been implemented. It is also important to point out that the threat picture against Norway is complex and that a one-sided focus on object protection may go beyond the protection of soft targets and cyberattacks by state actors. Much of the intelligence activity targeting Norway takes place in the digital space. In general, this activity is about collecting information, and the actors have both the ability and the ability to carry out cyberattacks. With regard to the terrorist threat, in its annual threat assessment, the PST has assessed it as ‘possible that attempted terrorist attacks will occur’. There are particularly soft targets that are exposed. A majority of the control committee has assembled on a proposal expressing distrust of the government. In the opinion of the Progress Party, the motion of censure is part of a political game in which the red-green parties should be too good to participate. As we in the Committee have gained insight into the work on this matter, there is no doubt that the government will deliver. There is no doubt that the preliminary work done by the previous government was too poor, and I remember that they even informed the Storting that this work ‘should not involve significantly increased costs’. Today we have the fasit. There is also something special to read in a committee position that the opposition must refer to a heated debate on the Eighth Day to try to justify its distrust of the government. It testifies to the fact that there are very bad arguments for this mistrust. We're now on the right track. It is provided for the safety and security of our citizens. There are several billion dollars invested in actions, to which I have referred. So we're talking here about securing far beyond the building of the property to the police and the defense, as opposed to what the Prime Minister and the Minister of Justice claimed just before the summer. We talk about the nation's most critical functions: politics, management, police, food, communication, water, defence, health and well-being – to name a few. The first report from the Riksrevision in the autumn of 2016 came down as a lightning strike in political Norway. When Stortinget dealt with saka in the spring of 2017, there was strong criticism. However, management is an important part of the conclusion of the Gørv report.» The follow-up report from the Riksrevision came in summer 2018. In the police, a lack of track record of time fixing when a person would be in line with the Security Act – loosely mapped to within 10 years, at the earliest, depending on funding. Rather, there is a lack of action, leadership and implementation that characterises the two highly critical reports, the latter concluding on the Riksrevision's very strict approach. ‘Very serious’ in four of the main conclusions of the report is perhaps the Riksrevision’s strictest sentence on a sitting government ever. And according to the National Audit Office, the notion of ‘very serious’ is only used for ‘conditions in which the consequences for society or the citizens concerned are very serious, such as the risk of life or health’. The National Audit Office's report, the hearings and replies from the government show that the government has not prioritised giving money to terrorist security in the police and the defence, despite the clear emphasis of the Stortinget after July 22 that it was important that a target was reached by 2015. The cause is no less serious because Stortinget and the Norwegian people have lived in uncertainty about what has happened. The studies show that for the government, none of the reports were any surprises, as they claimed in the March 2017 control hearing. They had been continuously briefed through supervisory reports, reporting and budgetary dialogues with both police and defence. For the Stortinget and the rest of the Norwegian public, it was not to be expected that one had not arrived more than seven years after the events in 2011. Only two out of 12 buildings in the police were then secured in line with the regulations. The net was also not secure. The Ministry of Justice and Defence does not listen to the Police Directorate on this matter and instead chose to continue to misinform the Stortinget. Furthermore, we can state that the government has not prioritised the security work. The Police Directorate asked for money in the budget dialogue in 2013, in 2014, in 2015, in 2016 and in 2017 to come to an end, but did not receive this from the Justice and Security Department because a priority was given to other matters over it. This admits the department itself in the report. The basic protection of displayable objects is not a priority in the budget. Other considerations were considered more important. It has also been found that the government has misinformed the Storting about the many and large holes in the follow-up to decisions, laws and instructions on object protection. The attitude of the Supervisory Committee to be debated and voted on today is based on the third round of the Riksrevision with criticism in this matter. This means that after two reports in which the Riksrevision points out serious deficiencies in the follow-up to object protection, and two rounds of consideration of the Riksrevision criticism in the Stortinget, the government had not taken the necessary steps to meet the requirements. The National Audit Office calls the findings ‘very serious’. It is the highest severity of criticism in the National Audit Office’s reports. According to the National Audit Office, there are so many shortcomings that it can have serious societal consequences, with a risk to life and health. The fact that the government has also misinformed Storting about the matter makes it even worse. For example, in the 2016 budget of the Ministry of Justice and Defence, which was presented in October 2015, it is named. att ‘all activities in the justice sector have reported that protective features are secured under the regulation on object safety’. The current Minister of Justice Anders Anundsen repeated the same claim in his reply to a written question from Stortinget in April 2016. Then the government blamed much of this misinformation on the Police Directorate. And it is true that POD provided a minor error information in 2014, but it only applied to securing forces, not basic protection and not to object protection as a whole. And: POD itself notified the government of this error, without the government giving the Storting clear information about this error. In addition, the government received information from several teams over a long time that it was far behind in object protection during that period. For example, the classified report of the National Audit Office shows: the police were in vain asking for more money to secure sheltered construction and infrastructure in 2013, in 2014 and 2015. Nor has the government fulfilled its obligation to provide information to Stortinget in a satisfactory manner in terms of object protection in the Defence. The Riksrevisions report Document 3:11 states, inter alia: the following: ‘The Ministry of Defence’s plan to establish basic safeguards for the shielding objects by 2025 is not in line with Storting’s decisions and assumptions.’ One thing is that the Ministry of Defence did not come to an end by 2015, as intended, but the most incomprehensible thing here is that the department starts operating with the 2025 year, ten years later than the deadline that was foreseen, without informing the Storting about it. During the hearing on object security on 27 August this year, Foreign Minister Eriksen Soreide was asked several times about when, as Minister of Defence from 2013 to 2017, she was informed that the Ministry of Defence first saw separately to comply with the basic security requirements in the Defence in 2025. Red also asked written questions as to why Eriksen Soreide did not inform Stortinget about this huge aftermath. In the reply we received, Minister of Defense Bakke-Jensen confirmed that the government could have been clearer to the Stortinget. We have not yet received a satisfactory answer from Eriksen Soreide as to why in 2015, when the deadline expired, she did not inform the Stortinget that the government was still years away from following what was the Stortinge's assumption. Furthermore, misinformation and the retention of important information in the subject matter of object protection are intertwined in a wide range of cases of what we consider to be a breach of the government's obligation to provide information to the Storing on security policy issues. We see similarly in the case of the satellite project, where the government asked the Stortinget for money for satellite broadband, without informing the Stortinget that the money would go to a project that also meant connecting the US offensive nuclear war systems in the northern areas to Norwegian civil satellites, or that we have seen with the US-led war adventure in Syria, where the government still refuses to disclose which armed militia groups one has used Norwegian defence resources to train and support in the war in Syria. In sum, the breach of Storting's assumptions, along with incorrect information and retention of information in the object protection case, is enough for us to support the proposal that the Storting regrets the government's action in the matter, and requires that something like that not happen again. At the same time, we support the proposal, with some reservations. The lack of object protection is not the sole responsibility of the current government. The Stoltenberg government made some unrealistic assumptions that the new object protection requirements would not cost anything. Neither did the red-greens make a good enough effort in the field during their reign. Red will also not uncritically benefit from the thinking behind the extensive and costly object protection, because we fear that maximum protection of hard targets can be done in a way that, in worst cases, could go beyond securing soft targets, i.e. civilian targets. With soft or civil targets, we think in particular of digital infrastructure, connecting points for physical infrastructure such as water, electricity, sewerage and passenger and goods transport. This is probably today the most likely target of unwanted actions intended to affect our country and is not necessarily adequately protected by today’s preparedness concept. In 2016, Chief of Defense Haakon Bruun-Hanssen came up with an opinion that we do not miss when we talk about the government's down-priority of object protection. Bruun-Hanssen then said that participation in war operations abroad has gone at the expense of domestic preparedness. We believe that this reflects an unacceptable prioritisation of our resources. In the last 20 years, Norwegian politicians from changing governments have implemented a drastic conversion of Norwegian politics, from peace nation to war nation, through participation in a number of foreign war operations, more and more of them under the command of the United States and NATO. The fact that Red is against it is not unknown, but when even the head of defense says that these war operations are at the expense of domestic preparedness, the alarm bells should ring for all those who care about a safe Norway. Unfortunately, this is a lesson that this government seems to have difficulty learning. Only a few months after the deadline for complying with the defence object protection requirements expired in 2015, the then Secretary of Defense – Eriksen Soreide – replied yes to sending Norwegian soldiers out on a mildly complicated US-led military mission in the unbridled Syria. The same thing happened earlier this year when the government, after being informed by the National Audit Office about another forthcoming report on the lack of prioritisation of preparedness, agreed to send Norwegian forces to Sahel in Africa. The government disposes of the population's tax money and the entire defence resources of Norway. These are the Community's resources, not Erna Solberg's own money, and they should be used to defend Norway and the Norwegian population, not in the US wars in the Middle East, Africa and Asia – wars that also create more problems than they solve. Red's demands are therefore simple: it is time for the government to put protection of Norway and the Norwegian population first.

Work shall also include analysis of the need for strength in different scenarios on the crisis scale. The time limit for the implementation of the work is set by the Directorate of Police by February 2019. I have noted the Riksrevision's assessments related to the lack of a common understanding of the object protection directive. I think it is important to clarify what the police director and the head of defence also pointed out in an open hearing, namely that there is no disagreement on how to interpret today's instructions. It is now more than six years since the instructions were laid down, and then it is quite natural to consider any changes. That is why, in consultation with the Ministry of Justice and Defence, the Ministry of Defence has set up a working group which is well under way to review the current instructions. The group will discuss, recommend and justify any changes to the instructions. Since 2013, this government has increased the appropriation by over 7 billion. kr to various preparedness measures in the justice sector, and the government has provided a history of police recruitment. In addition, bets are well over 1 billion. kr in 2019. A number of measures have been taken to strengthen police preparedness, including: a significant strengthening of the Police Security Service, strengthening the preparedness team, ordering three new police helicopters, the possibility of point-arming by vulnerable objects, demands for police response time, the establishment of a new annual national terrorist exercise and the establishment of a national emergency centre. I would like to stress that the government has taken seriously the assessments and recommendations of the previous report of the Riksaudition. I believe all the work we have done since 2015 shows that. We are also taking seriously the new recommendations for the Riksrevision. The instructions will be implemented during 2019 and the Police Directorate now has an obligatory plan that will ensure that all current protective features will be within the provisions of the new Security Act during 2020. Finally, I would like to remind you that the work on standby and object protection is a continuous work and something we will never be able to say is finally done. Protecting citizens is one of the most important tasks of a country, which this government has taken seriously and will continue to pay great attention to.

ParlaMint-NO_2018-12-05.ana.ud557e790	First, I would like to thank the Supervisory and Constitutional Committee for its extensive work on this position. This case extends, as I said earlier and as you know, back to 2015, when the Riksrevision made the first of its two inquiries. The majority has received two detailed reports from the Riksrevision. The matter has been debated in this Chamber earlier, and in the autumn the Committee has also received answers to 44 written questions that it has asked the Prime Minister, the Minister of Justice and Defence and the Minister of Defence. The Ministry has informed about the object protection work in its budget proposals to the Stortinget. We have recognised that we could inform more often, better and more fully about the work of strengthening object protection. The store should be given more clear information at an earlier time. This is a complex area involving several departments, several agencies and several institutions. It involves classified information and up-to-date information. Looking back, we had made a lot of money to inform better. As a contribution to this information work, I would like to go through the three most important aspects of the Ministry of Defence. I will first repeat some of the main points from the hearing in the autumn. I take the weaknesses the Riksrevision has found, in the greatest seriousness. I would like to stress that although there have been clear improvements since the first survey in 2015, there is still a lot of work to be done before the hedge is at a satisfactory level in all areas. I base the Riksrevision's findings and recommendations on further work. The Ministry of Defence commissioned the Defence and Defence Building to follow up on the National Audit Office's report shortly after it was presented in June. The government has already spent large sums on object protection, and substantial budgetary resources have been used in the coming years. The first of the three areas is the basic preventive protection of the defence of its own protective features. The National Auditor sees it as positive that we started a comprehensive investment project in 2016 to put the basic insurance system in place. At the same time, they point out that completion of this work will take a long time. We have a committed and funded plan to put the basic insurance in place. We have also followed up the Riksrevision's finding that there was a complete lack of an overview of the state of the defence objects. The defence was given such an overview before the open hearing in August. In addition, Defence Building works with an assessment of the specific technical security measures needed on the individual object. The establishment of basic insurance has proved to be far more complex, time- and cost-intensive than that envisaged by the government of Stoltenberg in 2010. When this government took over in 2013, there was not enough status overview of the hedge. We have therefore had to spend time getting a complete overview of the remaining security needs and building up the necessary skills across the defence sector to address what has proved to be a very complex area of concern. So the 2025 is not a change in a deadline in the regulation, but it's the year of the last planned payment in an investment program for securing displayable objects. We have been open that the plan did not meet the deadline in the regulation before it was started. The government has opened up financial support for the programme and has therefore set aside EUR 180 million. kr for 2019. That is why we have also announced that, in the context of the revised national budget, we should be able to respond to the practical feasibility of completing the work earlier than 2025. Significant funds are used for object protection in the defence sector. The programme already mentioned has a framework of 450 million. kr. The size has been made known to this sum through the budget proposals for 2018 and 2019. In addition, we have started, or are in the process of implementing, other basic insurance measures with a total cost framework of over 1.6 billion. kr. Only in Ørland, about 600 million are now invested. kr in new basic insurance measures. Then there is not only money here, but also the capacity to project and get regulated companies to implement the projects. Work must also be aligned with other high priority construction projects, such as Ørland and Evenes. It is important for me to make it clear that the defence objects are not unsafe. All objects have a form of basic protection. About half are in military camps, with fences and military guards. An object may have a number of safeguards, but as long as it does not have all the four categories of measures required by the current Safety Act, it is nevertheless assessed as unsatisfactoryly ensured. The new Safety Act makes more functional requirements for the fuse, which facilitates more accurate and cost-effective security measures. So for object protection with security forces: For the defence, this is assistance to police security and military security of key objects in war. The National Audit Office is quite clear that this work has been improved – and not just improved. The National Audit Office mentions several concrete examples of how it has improved, while pointing out that there are still significant challenges. I want to compare with the Riksrevision's findings for 2015. Then was the work of designating the civil key objects of the Defense, upon which the Defense depends in war, to the extent begun. But now all of these are largely designated. There was not enough quality of the information related to this work, but now there are good ICT solutions and up-to-date databases. At that time, there were weaknesses in the exchange of information and cooperation on planning by the police and the defence. It's now improved. At that time, the Home Guard had an object plan for less than half of the objects, as it is now approaching full coverage of the object plan. While weaknesses were identified in police/home security exercises, there is now agreement on at least one annual joint exercise or other competence action in each district. I still recognise the Riksrevision's observation that there is still a lack within this part of the object protection. We are now making improvements in the process of designating civil key objects, and the Defence Quality Assures its analysis of the Home Security capability to secure the objects. The quality assurance confirms so far prior information that the Home Protection system initially has the capacity to secure all the listed objects. The Riksrevision report states that when the Home Office also has to carry out wartime tasks other than object security, there will not be enough capacity to simultaneously secure both all the key objects and the pre-selected objects from the police. The National Audit Office’s assessment is consistent with the information provided during the hearing last year and this year on the capacity of the Home Protection Agency. At the same time, I remind you that there is no requirement for all objects to be secured simultaneously. The protection of the home and other parts of the defence is not dimensioned in order to perform all of their tasks simultaneously. The defence's object protection capacity is not limited to Home Protection, as it has created an impression in this case. The defence may also use other departments, other strengths for this job. During the hearings in the autumn, the Chief of Defence and the Police Director were aware that the object protection directive did not cause any difficulties in cooperation. The adjustment needs identified by the police have been included in the joint review of the currently ongoing instructions. The third main area is the National Audit Office’s observation of weaknesses in the operational capability of the Home Protection Agency. The government has been open that there are weaknesses in it. This has also been clearly demonstrated by the Chief of Defense’s assessment of operational capability, which is presented annually to the Stortinget. Although challenges remain for Home Affairs, I am pleased that Defense is now reporting a significant improvement in this operational capability. This is the result of a conscious effort on plans, materials, personnel and exercises. In the first part of the 2017-2020 planning period, we started a further modernisation of the Heimevernet. In 2017, the Home Guard budget was significantly strengthened with an additional budget of 191 million. to resume a long-term lag in maintenance, spare parts and emergency stocks. This is a continuation of the ambitions of the long-term plan, and the slowdown is now largely in place. The Home Office is now adapting the organisation to an area structure of 37 000 soldiers, in line with the Storting decision. In the revised national budget for 2018, the government proposed to strengthen Heimevertnert by 11 million. kr related to an increase in the area structure. We also proposed an increase of 25 million. kr for the faster acquisition of new clothing and equipment. For watchkeeping of seaside objects, the government would provide a smaller number of commercial small boats and markings and barges. At the same time, the aim is to establish a system for the requisition of civil ships to assist that service. I note that the majority of the Committee has not been willing to listen to the information on the operational capability of Home Affairs and the number of areas ready to respond. Instead, the Riksrevision report is used as a facilitator to establish that the Ministry of Defence misinformed the Stortinget. It's a strange way for me to deal with it that's part of the whole point of a hearing, namely to ensure counter-dictation and better case information. As the head of Homeland Protection said, the operational status of Homeland Protection has been steadily improving in recent years. This applies to the updating of plans, the introduction of materials, the upgrading of skills and the training and training of personnel. In the last five years, home protection has had a generally good status on exercise in relation to requirements. This was last tested during the NATO exercise in the fall, where well-equipped soldiers in the Home Guard trained for the organisation and reception of allied reinforcements. The tasks also included strength protection and protection of both pre-planned objects and objects for which there is no plan. The feedback from the exercise is that this was done in a very satisfactory way, and the Home Protection System was made visible that strength protection and object protection are something they can. For 2019, the government proposes to allocate 61 million. kr to increase the length of the annual exercise for the entire area structure – similar to what we do for HV-16 in Troms and HV-17 in Finnmark. This will also allow a greater share of the area structure to be practised annually. This will contribute to further improving the capability of the Home Protection System for object protection. So that we've given a makeup picture of the situation. I must be allowed to disagree with that. ParlaMint-NO_2018-12-05.ana.ud557e943	I would like to take this opportunity to repeat in this Chamber that we agree with the Riksrevision when it finds that it will still take a long time before protective objects in the police and the Defence have a permanent basic security that fully satisfies the desired level of security. We're taking this seriously. When the regulation on object safety was adopted in October 2010, it was considered that it would not have any significant administrative or economic impact. At the same time, a deadline for implementation was set. Today, we know that implementation has proved far more costly and time consuming than initially foreseen. With the knowledge we now have, we know that it was unrealistic to reach the prescribed deadline in the regulation, 1 January 2015, for all objects. Knowledge of the complexity and demanding object protection work has been a gradual recognition. The complexity and cost of the work are something my government should communicate earlier, more clearly and more collectively to Stortinget. I would like to stress from Stortinget's pulpit that I take self-criticism at this. In the Ministry of Justice's budget proposal for 2019, we have outlined the overall plan that has now been drawn up by the Police Directorate to close the deviations identified by the National Police Audit Office. The plan is that the residual risk of today’s shielding objects will be within the provisions of the new Security Act, as the Directorate understands the law, during 2020. The Government has proposed appropriations for 2019 to enable the Police Directorate to follow up the plan. For the defence objects, the Ministry of Defence launched in 2016 a comprehensive investment programme with a framework of 450 million. kr in order to provide basic security under the Safety Act. The defence has a very large number of shielding objects, and it is a time-consuming and competence-intensive work to monitor them and implement the necessary measures. An overall state of defence features has now been established. The Ministry of Defence has approved three sub-projects and further sub-projects are under consideration. The government has opened up an economic support for the programme and has set aside a total of 180 million. kr in 2019, and the possibility to carry out further work is now under review. I would like to stress that this is not necessarily the money that is the most scarce, but the human resources that are going to – people with a safety professional background who can project and plan the measures. Entrepreneurs must be safety-cleared, and at the moment we have many major building projects in the Defence Department built by clear security contractors because of the conversions and the construction that are being carried out. So we have to look at what it will be practical to get to. Therefore, we will come back in the context of the revised national budget for 2019 – as I also said in the hearing – with a better overview and status of this. Due to technological changes and planned closures, some defence features could fall out of the list of protective features in the coming years. That means that not all objects on today's list are going to be prioritised in the work of repairing their protective status. Anyway, I want to remind you that an object is not secured under the regulations, not that it is unsafe. This means that the objects may be secured by several different types of measures, even if the measures do not fully comply with the provisions of the regulation. They can also be guaranteed by redundancy, that is, that the function can be maintained by taking over it by another device, and thus that the purpose for which we are actually protecting it is maintained. The NSM Director pointed out in the hearing that the method also has an impact here. An anomaly may indicate a breach of a code, but it does not necessarily say how much is up or down. New objects can be established, other objects are phased out, and there may be new threat assessments that affect work, such as: have looked at cyber and ICT security in recent years. I'm saying this, because we don't want to be 100 pst. finished with something in this area because the threats will change, the objects will change. That's why we'll always have to work with object protection, because objects will go in and out of lists when the need changes in work. In the National Audit Office’s 2015 survey and in the 2017 hearing, there was a great deal of focus on the weaknesses in the police and defence work on property protection with security forces. I am pleased that the National Audit Office recognises the efforts made between the first and second revisions of this area, when they say that ‘the Ministry of Justice and Defence and the Ministry of Defence have improved the follow-up to the use of security forces by the police and the Defence.’ At the same time, the National Audit Office points out that there are still significant challenges in this area as well. We are therefore in the process of following up on the Riksrevision findings and recommendations related to the securing forces. It is important and right that the Riksrevision and the Control and Constitutional Committee have put object protection on the agenda. We know that social security and preparedness are a work we never finish, a work that requires constant attention from both government and the Storing. We therefore believe that we also need to look at the threats we face. The world is changing rapidly. That's why from the first day we got into government, we've been dealing with the whole spectrum of work on social security and preparedness, not just object protection. We have had a significant economic boost in Defence, in the police and in ICT, to mention something. The work on object protection will be given priority in the future, not only in the 2019 budget to which I have referred, but also in the future state budgets. The ministries will follow up the dialogue with their underlying agencies. We take over the criticism that has been raised from the Riksaudition, and the signals that we now get from Stortinget through the setting today. The staging has adopted a new security law. The implementation of it is followed up by the government, and we are in the process of laying down new regulations and bringing the law into force from 1 January 2019. In the work on new safety laws, it has been important to establish precisely that it is the function to be ensured and that cost-benefit assessments have to be made of the security measures. This has also been achieved by Stortinget through the treatment of the new Security Act. In the context of the implementation of the new law, new assessments will be made of both objects and protective measures, and this will be followed closely in the future. So object protection is a dynamic field, and it's a high priority field, but also in areas that are highly political priority, we have to balance. When the threat picture changes, our priorities need to change so that we are at the best we can stand up to today's threats. Of course, we need a long-term response to the major threats, to war and conflict, but we also need to focus on the most current and immediate threats. In recent times, simple but deadly attacks on soft targets have been a major threat in Europe. It has called for the strengthening of the PST, the police present, better training the police, close cooperation between the PST and the Intelligence Department. Finding this balance between preparedness for war and a serious crisis and preparedness for various types of terrorist attack is also part of being responsible as a government and responsible as a politician. I look forward to a good and constructive dialogue with the Stortinget on these demanding balances in the future as well. There is no doubt that our challenges are great, and that we need to make priorities on the road.

The National Auditorate wrote, among other things:\t\t You can change the ‘Neither the police nor the Defence, together or separately, will be able to exercise adequate protection of essential objects when a threat situation so warrants.’ The same gloomy main picture was then redrawn by the Riksrevision in Document 3:11 for 2017-2018, now based on a revision until 31 December 2017. Again, the Riksrevision concluded with ‘very serious’ criticism. That wording implies that there may be a risk to life and health. At the hearing of the Control and Constitutional Committee on 27 August this year, Prime Minister Erna Solberg admitted: ‘In terms of basic security, there is still a lot that has not been put in place’. The police director confirmed that the police are only in port with the protection of two out of twelve shielding objects. Today we hear new promises that this will be in place by 2020. With regard to the use of hedges, the effort is still far behind the size envisaged. There are still major questions about the operational capability of Home Affairs. There is a lack of clarification of the capacity of the police and the Home Office to protect objects through the use of security forces, and we still cannot be sure that the police and the Defence have a clear and common understanding of the important object protection directive. Against this background, Stortinget has to conclude that there are still serious shortcomings related to this work. It is very serious and provides a basis for regretting the government's failure to follow up work on object protection. Equally serious is the question of information to Stortinget from the government and the Prime Minister in the case in question. Once again, after July 22, we agreed that openness and honesty were crucial. Transparency was important in rebuilding confidence in the population that preparedness had improved. ParlaMint-NO_2018-12-05.ana.ud557e1438	Since accession in 2013, the government has been focused on improving security and enhancing preparedness. The government has improved the operational capability of the Home Office. Guards and fuses are the primary mission of the Home Guard and HV is the main part of the Defence Security Forces. But the government has not come as far as it wanted in terms of basic preventive protection of objects. The reason for this is that implementation has proved to be far more complicated, cost-intensive and resource-intensive than was foreseen when the regulation on object safety was laid down in 2010. The deadline set for 2015 in the regulation has proved unrealistic. In addition, the work on object protection has had little attention until 2013. Insufficient funding of defence over many years resulted in a large maintenance kit run, a shortage of spare parts and cut-offs in operational operations. This also affected the operational capacity of the Home Office. The long-term defence plan has reversed the trend. Step by step, the defense is built up. There is now consistency between the structure adopted by Stortinget for the Defence and the appropriations made available by Stortinget. The alternative has been a managed liquidation, as the defense chief Haakon Bruun-Hanssen characterized the development of the Defense as. The government has significantly increased the budget of Homeland Protection. The strengthening of HV started in the previous planning period and is continued and reinforced in this long-term planning period from 2017 to 2020. In 2017, the Heimevertet was granted an additional grant of 191 million. kr, just to pick up the maintenance trigger, purchase several spare parts and increase the emergency stock, as well as money for the purchase of anti-lock equipment, which has helped to increase the object protection capability of HV. The training activity in Home Protection has increased. Home security is getting a lot of new material – new uniforms, new weapons, new connections and new vehicles are being acquired. Recently, a contract was signed for the purchase of 360 new field wagons for the Home Protection Agency. In addition – and it is not mentioned in fact – tens of special vehicles are transferred from the Army to HV, which will be armed with heavy weapons and which provide HV with enhanced preparedness and, in fact, greater impact power. The Home Security budget in 2019 is expected to increase by 216 million. kr. It gives more practice to the soldiers, and it gives more new material. The duration of the annual training will in all HV regions up to the next year be equivalent to the duration of the exercise in the highest priority HV districts. In addition, several HV soldiers shall practise throughout the year. The Head of the Heimevernet, Major General Eirik Johan Kristoffersen, stated in the hearing of the Control and Constitutional Committee that: ‘The operational status of Home Protection has been continuously improving in recent years. Home security is increasingly being provided with material and ammunition in its emergency storage facilities. This material fulfillment is part of what is called the long-term pavement. The ambition is that the Home Guard should have filled its inventories with mission-critical material during 2018, with the exception of the material to be delivered in new projects. The filling of materials is absolutely necessary because, like the rest of the Defense, the Home Protection system has been in stock for many years without adequate refilling.’ The National Audit Office's survey concerns the protection of the property of the Home Office's territorial units. While the HV is the primary capacity of the defence within object protection, when assessing the overall security capability of the defence, the available capacities of other parts of the defence must also be considered. I mention above all the Home Guard's own effort forces, I mention the guard forces in the garrison, and I also mention elements from the Coast Guard. It is also not necessary to ensure that all listed objects can be secured simultaneously. In a real situation, it may be appropriate to ensure some selected objects. In this case, it must be based on a situation-specific assessment of the object to be given priority in the current situation and of the threats to be faced. The securing strengths may also be transferred between the districts. - Enough about HV. The National Audit Office's report is taken as seriously as possible and is used as the basis for the government's further work on object protection. The report points out shortcomings, but it also points to a positive development from the previous national audit report, which was presented in 2016. And not least: the report illustrates the complexity of this work. When the Ministry of Defence’s instructions on securing and protection in the use of securing forces came into force in 2012, the starting point was very demanding for the Defence. Overall defence plans as a basis for concreteising the right key objects were not up-to-date. The departments that were previously responsible for the planning, development and follow-up of the key objects were all closed down. In 2012, the existing list of defence key objects was reviewed and obsolete objects removed. After the object protection directive came, a new key point directive was established in spring 2013, where responsibility and tasks were shared in the Defence. Finally, there were empowerment initiatives, which were implemented in the period 2013–2015. Defence has updated its defence plans over several years following the new security policy situation. Similarly, NATO's plans for Norway and the North have been updated. It makes us today have a significantly better basis for identifying key objects, including civilians. Against this background, civil key objects have increased through 2016, 2017 and 2018. Closing the deviations in the Riksrevisions report of 2016 has been very demanding – I would say almost impossible. Substantial funds have been allocated to improve basic security in Defence, yes, in fact several billion dollars. The appropriations from Stortinget are not the critical factor in the implementation of better basic security in the Defence so as to comply with the Security Act and the Security Act. It is necessary to set realistic timeframes for the implementation of such complex projects. The projects require special expertise. Special competence is required when describing the need. Special competence is required when projects are to be projected. These professional environments are small and limited in Norway, and those who are going to work on this need to be cleared up for safety. It takes time, it also takes time to contract, and physical work of this variety also takes time to carry out – no matter how quickly you want it to go. The implementation of object safety measures and the speed to be achieved are also determined by other laws and regulations to be observed in the project implementation. If we don't do that, we're going to give food to the National Audit Office.
