ParlaMint-NO_2018-12-05.ana.ud557e2131	The big message we are handling today is a document that is a transparent road map, and the document is also easy and easy to find out. The attitude is unanimous, but I also want to explain the majority comments, and then I expect the parties that have other comments to explain them. The Committee has examined the notification. St. 17 for 2017-2018, Partner countries in development policy, which answer several questions regarding Norwegian aid policy, including those addressed by the Request Decision 69 and by the Request Decision 72. The Committee would point out that the partner country concept, which forms the basis of the current notification of aggregates, was set out in the Notice. St. 24 for 2016-2017, Joint responsibility for the common future, referred to as the Development Communication, but the concept is further developed in the present Communication under consideration by the Committee. The partner country concept has been developed within the framework of the 2030 agenda and the 17 sustainability goals that the world has gathered around during the UN Summit back in 2015. The overall ambition of sustainability goals is that by 2030 all the countries of the world should have eradicated extreme poverty, secured inclusive development and promoted prosperity, peace and justice. The Committee has also stressed in its work that sustainability objectives cover all countries and all parts of society. Norwegian efforts for international assistance have increased sharply in recent decades, and the aid budget has increased steadily, with increasing appropriations and a political desire to lead in international development cooperation. The Committee also sees that the government has concentrated the Norwegian aid exposure on fewer countries, sectors and partners, in order to ensure that Norwegian efforts deliver actual, sustainable and measurable results. The number of beneficiary countries for Norwegian aid has been higher than 100 individual countries since 1994, but this has been reduced since 2014 and in 2017 the figure was 84 countries. The Committee points out that the leading role of Norway in other actions, such as the Climate and Forest Initiative, has also influenced the direction and mechanism of Norwegian aid. Sub-Saharan Africa has been an important region of Norwegian aid and will remain so in the years to come. Furthermore, we have seen in the Committee in our work that there are different criteria and classifications. Once introduced, to achieve concentration of Norwegian aid in the past, the number of countries receiving Norwegian aid has been relatively constant since the mid-1980s. The introduction of the partner country concept is seen as a good and possible opportunity to review which countries Norway should concentrate resources on in order to get the most out of the assistance. The introduction of the concept will not necessarily lead to the phase-out of assistance to other countries, but will mean that the government will develop wider and deeper cooperation with partner countries over time. There are some criteria on which the Committee has taken note when selecting partner countries. It is about reciprocity – that partnerships should be based on a mutual desire for cooperation for development within a common understanding of development objectives and priorities. It is also a joint responsibility which is the criterion for the 2030 agenda with the 17 sustainability goals and The Addis Ababa Action Agenda, which is the global consensus on how to finance the work to achieve precisely the sustainability objectives, and where it is established that each country has the primary responsibility for its own development and to mobilise national resources to achieve its objectives. Dialogue on the coherence of different policy areas, in particular with regard to human rights, equality, climate and the environment and anti-corruption considerations, has also been one of the key issues under consideration in this work, as well as development policy instruments in partner countries managed in a comprehensive perspective, a clearer arrangement of assistance to partner countries and with the aim of ensuring that partner countries have access over time to the breadth of Norway’s foreign policy tools, in close needs analysis.

We have also seen that elections have been held in a number of countries in recent years. That's good. But political choices have also been used in recent years by authoritarian leaders to just strengthen their power. The elections have not been real, because the freedom of the press has not been present, or the rule of law is politically ruled. I think that also has to have implications for our development policy. We have to be ambitious without becoming unrealistic. Today, we also promote proposals that the government should come back to the Grand Duchy with a comprehensive presentation of how the instruments of Norwegian development and assistance policy should be used to strengthen its own capacity and capacity to facilitate sustainable, inclusive, sustainable growth and, above all, fair distribution. Norwegian development policy should focus on areas where we have expertise in Norway, where we have experience and technology, and where it can benefit developing countries, for example. in oil management – where we have a lot to contribute – renewable energy and also fisheries. We need to show that we understand the developing countries' own priorities, while recognising that aid has the greatest effect when directed towards countries that show real willingness to help themselves. ParlaMint-NO_2018-12-05.ana.ud557e2275	Norway has helped to improve the lives of millions of people in recent decades. At the same time, we have failed to contribute to fundamental social change. All countries need to take ownership of their own challenges, mobilise their own resources and invest more in their own populations. Priority needs to be given to the contribution of sustainable and fair tax systems that can contribute to the transfer of resource resources to increased tax revenues. Priority needs to be given to the basis for long-term growth and development, education and health for both individuals and society. Over the next few weeks, we will develop country strategies for each partner country, with clear targets for what we will achieve through our presence. Professional cooperation through the Knowledge Bank, work on civil society and human rights, stronger private sector presence, multilateral involvement and bilateral aid cooperation is becoming important in our approach. The sixteen partner countries are divided into two categories: ten long-term partner countries and six partner countries for stabilisation and conflict prevention. The ten partner countries for long-term development are countries where Norway has had a broad and long-term commitment and where we have competence and capacity. At the same time, there are considerable variations between countries in this category. Malawi and Nepal face major poverty challenges and need our support to build capacity and to meet the most basic human needs. Indonesia, on the other hand, is a developing country, but would like to acquire specific competence to realise its potential. In Colombia, we had a central role in the negotiation of the peace agreement and are now a significant contributor to its implementation, while in Ghana, capacity building, competence transfer and Norwegian business cooperation are the main components of our cooperation. Among the six countries for stabilisation and conflict prevention, there are also some variations. For partner countries as a whole, Norway is in a position to make a difference and partnerships are based on reciprocity and a common understanding of objectives and priorities. I am pleased to note that there is a broad consensus on the broad lines of Norwegian development policy and broad support for the communication in its entirety. Finally, let me inform you that the United Nations migration platform will be dealt with before Christmas in New York. The Minister for Foreign Affairs has therefore asked to explain the matter in Stortinget on Thursday 13 December.

ParlaMint-NO_2018-12-05.ana.ud557e2499	I can say on behalf of the committee that the treatment of Prop. 99 L for 2017-2018 has been a short and painless process. There will therefore be a brief presentation of the Committee's position. The background to the bill is that the Stortinget has agreed to transfer first line tasks from the cultural memory field from the Riksantikvar to the county municipalities from 1 January 2020. In this respect, the Ministry has drawn up proposals for a new regulation on powers, etc. by the Culture Memory Act. The regulation must therefore enter into force on 1 January 2020. New regulations include, inter alia: that several first-line tasks are transferred from the Riksantikvar to the counties. Section 28 of the Cultural Memory Act does not authorise transfers of authority for all paragraphs of the Act, so the Ministry proposes that the paragraphs of the Cultural Memory Act, which are subject to delegation to the county municipalities, be added to the first paragraph of Section 28 of the Cultural Memory Act. The amendment to Section 28 is a prerequisite for the adoption of the regulation. The proposal involves the inclusion of Sections 10, 19, 20 and 22 in the layout of Section 28. A unanimous committee endorses this legislative proposal.
ParlaMint-NO_2018-12-05.ana.ud557e2510	Prop. 99 L for 2017-2018, Changes in cultural heritage [[girl is the right image according to the law]] The role of the county council as regional cultural memory authority is strengthened by the reform. The size has agreed that first line tasks in the cultural memory field are to be transferred from the Riksantikvaren to the county council, cf. Report. St. 22 for 2015–2016, New elected regions – role, structure and tasks, and Prop. 84 S for 2016-2017, New breakdown of the regional population elected level. The Ministry has followed this up by drawing up proposals for a new regulation on authority under the Cultural Memory Act. This Regulation shall enter into force on 1 January 2020. Regulation (EU) No 182/2011 of the European Parliament and of the Council of 11 February 2011 laying down the rules and general principles pursuant to the Culture Memory Act will be repealed. The principle of unity in the administration means that the State does not, without authority, delegate powers to the county councils as an autonomous elected body. The authority of the county councils under the Cultural Memory Act shall thus be ‘directly located’ in the county municipalities and shall not be delegated to them. Under the first paragraph of Section 28 of the Cultural Memory Act, the King determines who is the right authority in accordance with the provisions of the Law. Some of these provisions are listed in Section 28 but not all. Therefore, in order for authority to be transferred to the county councils as the regional reform requires, the list in Section 28 needs to be expanded with several provisions of competence. The provisions in question are §§ 10, 19, 20 and 22. Following the amendment of the law, these four paragraphs will be included in the list in Section 28 of the provisions under which the King may determine who is the right authority under the law. The legislative amendment is a prerequisite for the Ministry of Climate and Environment to adopt a new regulation on authority under the Cultural Memory Act, which is to transfer authority and tasks to county councils in the context of the regional reform. In order to implement the principle that assignment of tasks to elected bodies should have sufficient legal basis, the respective provisions of competence must be included in the list in the first paragraph of Section 28 of the Code of Cultural Memory.

ParlaMint-NO_2018-12-05.ana.ud557e2550	Thank you to the Committee for good cooperation. The proposal by the representatives of Henriksen, Aasrud, Torve, Sandberg and Grande calls for better organisation for top athletes and education. The Committee held an open hearing on the matter on 25 October in which NISO and the Olympic Summit took part. NISO wants, inter alia: the establishment of common national guidelines for the organisation of the study, adapted to the everyday life of a top-ranking student, and the commitment of all Norwegian higher education institutions and universities. The Olympic Summit wants students with top sports status to have a statutory right to have the studies organised. The Olympic peak also wants to demand 50 pst. study progress is modified so that lower-progress students can apply for study support according to the study progress they have. The Olympic Summit further wants the maximum time of eight years of study support to be removed. The Committee is aware that top-level athletes are a group that can have specific challenges in combining professional and training and believes that ensuring all access and opportunities to obtain education must be an objective of education and competence policy. The Committee believes that it is important that not only educational institutions but also sports federations facilitate higher education and the completion of a degree by top athletes. The Committee points out that the Olympic Summit already has special agreements with 18 different educational institutions for the 2018–2019 academic year, where parts of it are online studies. The Committee would stress that such individual adaptations should not be at the expense of the professional quality of training or by reducing the requirements of competence. Adjustments for individuals must also not be at the expense of the Community's needs or impair the quality of training provision. The majority of the Committee, the members of the Right Party, the Left Party and the Christian People's Party, refer to the opinion of the Council of State on 22 August 2018, where it appears that the government is in the process of reforming its competences, where part of the work is to explore measures that can make education support schemes more flexible and thus more relevant to those who are not traditional full-time students. However, the majority of the Committee, all except the members of the Workers' Party, emphasises that other groups also face similar challenges. If a scheme is introduced specifically for top athletes, more groups of beneficiaries may also be expected to request similar schemes. The majority point to the fact that people with disabilities currently also have special rights in the absence of study organisation and could benefit from a more general scheme including reduced study burden requirements in order to receive support from the Loan Fund.

ParlaMint-NO_2018-12-05.ana.ud557e2814	In the last ten years, the number of students travelling abroad, either for a semester or two or to complete a full degree, has increased by a total of 44 pst. Global trends in combination with national bets have led to increased student mobility. In 2017, approx. 23 500 exchange and full-time students from Norway to study abroad in higher education. 6,900 of these were exchange students. Norwegian exchange students are distributed across 99 different countries, and the geographical spread is greater than for degree students. As ten years ago, the three English-speaking major acts of education — the United States, Australia and the United Kingdom — attracted most exchange students from Norway. The majority of students have completed a degree abroad — a total of 16,600 completed a full degree abroad in 2017. These students should also be counted on when we talk about student mobility and internationalisation, as they have valuable knowledge when they return to Norway, either to study further or to work. Despite the growing number of Nordics who choose to study abroad, since 2011, there has been an imbalance in the number of outbound students versus incoming students in Norway. Norway has had a higher growth rate of foreign students compared to other countries, despite high cost levels and getting well-known universities. In 2017, there were over 9 000 exchange students in Norway and a total of 34,300 exchange and full-time students who came to Norway to study. Norway has committed itself through the Bologna process to common quantitative objectives for student exchanges. The objective of the Bologna process is to ensure that all students who completed a graduate education in one of the Bologna countries in 2020 would have an exchange abroad during their education. The quality message set the objective of at least 20 pst. of those completing a degree in 2020 in Norway shall have been in exchange. In the long term, the goal is 50 pst. of all students, have had a study stay abroad, as defined in the Annual General Report Culture for Quality in Higher Education from 2017. In recent years, the proportion of candidates with exchange experience has been relatively stable, around 15 p.m., but with wide variations between disciplines, study types and institutions. According to the latest Eurostudent survey, 13 pst informed. of the students that they have had a study stay abroad during their current study programme. Plus 21 pst. exchange plans. On the other hand, over 65 pst. by the students, no plans to go abroad. It will require a significant effort to achieve the 20 pst target. exchange. So what's going to be a long term measure of 50 pst? require exchange? And why is it important that more students leave for a shorter or longer period? As citizens of a globalised world, there is little doubt that both students and working life can benefit from international perspectives and intercultural competence as well as professional knowledge. The yield in terms of language and cultural competence will, of course, increase with increasing duration of the stay and is therefore more extensive for full-time students. Knowledge of the culture of others is also likely to develop more tolerance for other cultures. International student mobility is one of several instruments for internationalisation in education and internationalisation shall be a means of enhancing the quality of education. However, not all students have the opportunity or wish to leave during the study. Internationalisation at home means that Norwegian students receive international impulses in their usual study environment. This may happen, for example: through the internationalisation of the curriculum and/or the integration of international students into Norwegian student environments. Digitalisation also opens up new opportunities such as virtual forms of cooperation and, for example, joint study programmes and training between different countries. Study stays abroad can give the individual student – and ideally also the study environment at home – other impulses. Therefore, both exchanges and exchanges of students are important. But if we want more people to get out, we have to remember that the threshold can be high for students who feel that they are standing very alone in figuring out what opportunities they have, and which subjects can be accepted to an existing degree. The students themselves state that there are three factors that motivate them to leave: that they receive good and relevant information in a timely manner, that the exchange does not go beyond the study process and that the exchange provides good learning outcomes both professionally and in view of more generic competences such as language and cultural understanding. There will therefore be a great deal to be recovered from the fact that the institutions themselves are making the best possible use of the student choice of good exchanges. This can be done, inter alia: by facilitating the exchange of one semester of the study programme and establishing a professionally anchored and quality assurance agreement with partner institutions. For planned programmes, such as: the professional study, it is all the more important to find good topics from foreign partners that can be included in their own way and that any practice is adapted or can be accepted when the students return to Norway. For more and more students, when they return to Norway, they do not have part or all of their education approved. This is a special case for studies that require authorisation at home. Although the Loan Fund provides support for the education you want to take and the NOKUT has approved the apprenticeship, there is no guarantee that the students get jobs with the profession they have trained for when they come home. Here it is a great potential for improvement. Students should be able to obtain prior approval from their own institutions on subjects to be included in a Norwegian degree before travelling abroad. Furthermore, both the Loan Fund and the intermediary of full-time studies abroad should be more responsible for which studies are approved and recommended to the Norwegian youth. ParlaMint-NO_2018-12-05.ana.ud557e2988	The Committee has noted that in Prop. 6 L for 2018-2019 it is proposed to extend § 8 g of the Road Traffic Act in order to provide a basis for electric and hydrogen-powered motor vehicles not to pay more than half of the price of parking compared to petrol and diesel-powered vehicles in all or certain charging municipal parking spaces. It is also proposed to repeal the fee support for the processing of the self-notification form and to provide a basis for the sectoral charging for the supervision of parking establishments. This means changes to Section 8(a) and (d). Until 1 January 2017, municipalities were required to offer free parking to electric and hydrogen-powered motor vehicles in municipal parking spaces marked with public traffic signs. Then I would like to comment on the matter I have now submitted as the rapporteur-general. First of all, the centre party will vote in favour of changes in § 8(a) and (d), but we will vote against changes in § 8(g). The reason for this is that the central party's starting point in this case is that we cannot have a symbolic and centralized climate policy that does not take account of differences in geography and settlement and that shifts costs to the municipalities. When I say this, I know that someone would say that the center party is not concerned about the environment, but it is wrong. The center part is concerned about the environment, and we think emission-free cars are super. The centre party believes that there should be good advantages to the purchase of emission-free cars. Then we get speed in the replacement of the car park. But one fact in this case is that the current legislation has only worked for barely a year. And all municipalities already have the opportunity to provide a reduced price for a zero-emission vehicle if the municipality sees that it is the right tool in their local environment. We know that municipalities take environmental responsibility. As an example, Bodø works hard to become a new smart environment city. Bodø wants, among other things: become Norway’s first city without bus emissions. There may be a better bet on the environment than half the parking price for an electric car. And maybe Bodø will also prioritize free parking for electric cars? I don't know. The central party believes that the municipality itself must be able to control these priorities. Municipal self-government is important. This bill on the differentiation of municipal parking goes against the freedom of municipalities to choose the best solution for themselves, based on a local assessment. I want to refer to NOU 2016: 4, New municipal law, where the committee proposes to legislate municipal autonomy. A change of laws like this, about these municipal parking spaces, is against this intention. One fact is that increased traffic in cities, including by electric cars, can replace public transport, bicycles and corridors. It can lead to more particulate matter and more land set aside for traffic purposes. Electric cars are good, but sometimes it may be better to stimulate the use of e.g. emission-free buses and more public transport instead. It is also a fact that this bill discriminates between municipalities and other actors offering parking. If the government's conviction is that the price of parking zero-emission cars is what determines whether we achieve the climate objectives, it would be more real to introduce this principle in all parking spaces, not restrict the authority of municipalities and at the same time allow private operators to maintain the right of self-evidence over their parking spaces. It also takes equal competition into account. Now I haven't read all the recordings that have come to this hearing, but very many of those who have made hearings have been critical of the proposal. They think it's an ad hoc proposal that's come up very suddenly, and they're reluctant to introduce it. If it is introduced, we believe that all parking spaces should have the same.
ParlaMint-NO_2018-12-05.ana.ud557e3013	Norway today appears to be one of the world’s leading countries for electric vehicles. This is because we have arranged for it through both the general charging system, free passage in the roadblocks and free parking. In addition, access to public transport has been important at this stage. As the number of electric cars has increased, especially in urban areas, it has become necessary to change this. In Act 4 September 2015, the obligation of the municipality to offer free parking spaces to electric and hydrogen-powered cars was lifted. This provision entered into force on 1 January 2017. After that, several municipalities have turned up the tariffs. When examining the 2017 State Budget, it was decided to establish a national binding rule that a zero-emission vehicle should not pay more than a maximum of 50 pst. of conventional vehicle tariffs in the case of tolls, ferries and parking spaces. In order to implement this for parking, it is necessary to amend Section 8 of the Road Traffic Act. In its decision, the size assumes that the choice of environmentally friendly solutions still has to be economically and practically viable. A ceiling was therefore set on how much one could charge in terms of tolls, ferry tariffs and parking fees. The 50 pst rule meets this. However, it is important to stress that the need for change varies greatly from region to region. It must therefore be up to the local authorities to determine for themselves where on the scale 0 to 50 pst. one should lie down. This will provide better and more effective supervision. The Ministry also proposed to repeal the State Road Service’s decision to charge a fee for the processing of the self-notification form for the parking establishment. An own-account notification system is used that does not require administrative resources, and thus there is no need to charge for the task. A change to the Road Traffic Act as proposed will reduce the urban space without giving good reasons for the benefits that will be realised as a result of the proposal. ParlaMint-NO_2018-12-05.ana.ud557e3130	For patients in need of complex and long-term coordinated services under the specialist health service law, a coordinator shall be provided, cf. specialist health service law § 2-5 a. This obligation to appoint a coordinator came in January 2012 and replaces previous rules on a patient-responsible doctor. According to the Directorate-General for Health, the coordinator shall ensure the necessary follow-up of the individual patient. The coordinator shall also ensure coordination of the provision of services in connection with the suspension of institutions and other service providers and ensure progress in the work on an individual level. The health service coordinator's tasks involve coordinating internally during the stay and externally with those to follow up after the job is finished. If the need for continuity and consistency in the course warrants it, the coordinator shall interact with personnel and bodies outside the institution who have or will have a responsibility for the patient for the treatment or follow-up. Examples are given of this, such as the local authority, the local authority coordinator, home health care or specialists in other health care agencies. The coordinator must interact with contact doctors about patients who have been appointed to it, and it is the health care provider’s coordinating unit that has overseen the responsibility for the appointment, training and guidance of the coordinator. It is natural that the appointment of the coordinator takes place at the department that has the day-to-day responsibility for the patient. These are clear requirements for healthcare professionals on how to perform the coordinator’s function. In an interpellation debate, the representative, Bent Höie, announced in June 2017 that former Minister of Health Ansgar Gabrielsen had as a hobby, when traveling around visiting hospitals and health care establishments, to ask about the system of coordinators. It was between 2004 and 2005, and it was rare that anyone could answer if this scheme was in place. In the debate on the interpellation, the representative of Høie said that he had inherited Ansgar Gabrielsen's hobby, simply to ask about this when he traveled around with the Health and Care Committee and visited various health care establishments. The reply was received by Høie, who was the same as Ansgar Gabrielsen. Mr. Stortingsrett Höie said in the 2017 interpellation that he had also asked the then Minister of Health Jonas Gahr Støre about how many patients actually received an appointed coordinator, and it did not receive a representative of the Groningensrettel Höie's response. So there was no national follow-up to this legal requirement. Mr. Stortingsretten Höie said in the storeroom that he meant this was serious, primarily for the patients, but also that the Stortinget had legalized something that was not followed up. I have inherited the hobby of Ansgar Gabrielsen and Bent Höie. I ask when I visit health care establishments and hospitals: how many patients have been appointed coordinator? They can't answer that, or they could say that it applies to the patients who are in a package process, but they don't know otherwise. I also asked the current Minister of Health if he can answer how many patients have been appointed coordinator, and I have received the same answer as he did. Health Minister Höie is unable to respond to the number of patients who have been appointed coordinator. In answer to question No. 15:744 in 2015 to Stortinget, Minister of Health Höie, states that he does not require specific reporting on compliance with legal requirements. The Department does not have an overview of which hospitals provide patients with the need for complex or long-term and coordinated services, a coordinator. The Ministry assumes that the enterprises meet the different requirements of the Special Health Services Act. Health Minister Höie has previously stated that if health professionals answer that they are in the process of creating a coordinator, he compares that if one of us is stopped by the police after running for 70 km/h in the 50 km/h zone, then we do not answer the police that we are in the process of getting down to 50 km/h. It won't be accepted, and then we don't believe in it, and it's not acceptable. This is also the case with the coordinator function. When it is legal for patients to be offered a coordinator, and that does not happen, that is unacceptable. So to specialist health care professionals: This right, which is also permitted by the same law, shall apply to patients with serious illness, injury and suffering who need treatment and follow-up over time. When the Stortinge dealt with this system of contact doctors for patients with serious illness, the Council of State stated that the amendment of the law would also clear up aspects of the coordinating arrangement that did not work. In a video report from Dagens Medisin on November 8th this year, there were several clear examples that the contact medical system is not working properly either. The report follows a patient with severe neck injury and a course in which he is treated in four different hospitals. For the first time, the patient was not informed from any hospital that he was entitled to a contact doctor, but even discovered that the right was introduced by chance. He himself had to submit a complaint before he got a contact doctor. The patient in today's medical report also did not have a contact doctor appointed for the entire patient's course. The report he received from his rehabilitation hospital, Sunnås hospital, was that the contact doctor was only a contact person for the treatment at this one hospital. In the respondent's letter to the patient, Sunnå's hospital writes: The patient may have two contacts at the same time as the patient in the specialist hospital, one at the emergency hospital and one at the rehabilitation hospital. The hospital writes further: You have received treatment in several hospitals. Despite the fact that such cases are absolutely not uncommon, this issue is not directly addressed in the Directorate-General for Health of the Liaison Medical System. When the Council of State proposed the introduction of the contact medical system, it was stated that seriously ill people and their relatives would be spared from stress by sticking to many different games and telling the medical history again and again. The patient’s need for a permanent contact doctor to stay at home is not diminished because if the patient is treated in several hospitals. On the contrary, it is likely more burdensome to stay informed about the course of treatment if one has to keep in touch with many different institutions. So if the patient himself has to know the law, it applies to both those who need a contact doctor and those who need a coordinator, I think it must be contrary to the intention behind the scheme. When the contact doctor scheme was being heard, the Ministry concluded that once it has been established that a patient is entitled to a contact doctor, the patient should be immediately aware of this. This situation requires answers from the Minister of Health to some questions: Has the establishment of the contact medical system cleared up the problems of the coordinator system, as represented by the Council of State, and has it led to the hospital meeting the obligation to appoint a coordinator to a greater extent? Does the Council have an overview of the number of hospitals that do not fulfil the obligation to appoint a contact doctor for seriously ill patients? Does the Council of State think that it is sufficient for seriously ill patients treated in several hospitals to be appointed a contact doctor at every hospital? And what is being done to clear up the ambiguities that exist in the specialist consultancy on this issue? Will the Council of State require healthcare professionals to have an overview of how many patients are actually offered to the coordinator, and whether they actually meet the legal requirements, or does it, as other health ministers before, assume that this is up to healthcare professionals themselves to offer it, and that the Storting does not need to get an overview?

ParlaMint-NO_2018-12-05.ana.ud557e3165	I am pleased to note that I still share the representative's commitment to these arrangements. Unfortunately, it is not with pleasure that I also partly share the representative's frustration. Whole and coordinated treatment provision has been a requirement of patient and user organisations for many years and is central to the development of the patient’s healthcare. The government has implemented several measures to improve coordinated services. We have called for longitudinal coordinators in the cycle of cancer, mental health and drug abuse, and we have introduced a law on contact doctors for patients with serious illness, injury or suffering who need to be followed up in the specialist healthcare service over time. We have also made clear the provisions on the coordinator in the specialist health service. Health regions are working systematically to implement these requirements, and I have recently received feedback on the status of this work. All health establishments in all regions have established coordinating units. Health North and Health West have also established regional coordinating units and allocated resources to this. Health enterprises have developed guidelines for the functions of the coordinating unit, coordinators, contact doctors and individual level work. Laws and guides are well known to the coordinating units. However, some health professionals indicate that managers in the line have not received sufficient information about the supervisors, and that there is generally little demand and knowledge of them among those responsible for the patient. Health Nord has recently completed regional guidelines for the appointment and function of the coordinator. Health Mid-Norway has established a regional procedure for the function of a contact doctor and has worked to coordinate procedures between health establishments. Health Vest has developed broad guidelines for the individual level and coordinator of all health enterprises in cooperation between regional coordinating units and coordinating units of health enterprises. However, the extent to which the schemes have been implemented varies in the different hospital departments. Although solutions have been developed at system level, the departments may land on different solutions, depending on the capacity, competence and type of subject area. The requirements for the coordinator and contact physician must be adapted to the good arrangements already in place, such as: Ambulance team, coordination nurses for individual diagnostic groups and printing coordinator. Health enterprises have limited knowledge of the number of patients covered by the schemes. They state as a reason that the EPJ system does not have sufficient functions to record which patients have been appointed coordinator and contact doctor. Health companies in Health Mid-Norway have worked systematically to obtain data related to these schemes, and they can now collect reports on the number of coordinators appointed. The reports show that mainly patients in the fields of mental health protection and drug abuse, rehabilitation and rehabilitation are appointed coordinators. In 2018, I requested that health regions should establish a registration system in the patient administration systems for patients that had been assessed and, if necessary, assigned to a contact doctor. During the reporting in June, health regions informed that a common procedure had been established for recording the proportion of patients with designated contact doctors in the DIPS and that health care establishments are working to implement the procedures for registration. This registration system may also be used for patients who have been awarded a coordinator. Health Bergen has taken out a report that shows that 741 patients were assigned contact doctors in 2017. Until 10 June 2018, 904 patients had been assigned a contact doctor and a total of 3,474 patients were actively covered by the scheme. The Health Directorate has assessed the extent to which healthcare has been able to implement the contact medical system by asking 512 employees of healthcare companies and private non-profit hospitals. Half of the respondents answered the questionnaire, and 70 pst. they replied that they were well in the process of implementing the scheme by their department, institution or subject area. They reported a lack of a good system to assign contact doctors as the main obstacle to the scheme. Secondly, the doctor’s work plans and internal disagreements on the type of resource use and that the scheme would lead to excessive workload for doctors. Several health professionals indicate that the system of longitudinal coordinators in the package processes has been easier to implement than the legal requirements of the coordinator and contact doctor. One important reason is that the longitudinal coordinator scheme is linked to defined patient groups and patient pathways. It is therefore appropriate to consider the link with patient trajectories also for the arrangements of the coordinator and contact physician. The package pathways for cancer, mental health and alcohol include a significant proportion of the target population for contact doctors, coordinators and individual levels. It is important to clarify the role of statutory arrangements in different areas of ongoing progress and coordination. It is also essential that the coordinators are anchored in management and that there is not too much responsibility for the individual coordinator, which will make the system vulnerable. The objection to the LMP is that it is too wide, in the sense that it includes patients who do not need a contact doctor or that it is practically difficult to implement the scheme. The recommendations of the supervisor on the patients to be included in the scheme need to be assessed and a safe system of contact between patients and contact doctors needs to be established – without prejudicing the role of the individual. For example, Project Westland Space opens so that contact doctors can have an electronic dialogue with the patient through a functionality on the website Westland Space. There is considerable work in healthcare companies to improve the coordination of patient processes so that patients can receive a safe and predictable treatment provision. But we need to increase the rate of change in this work, and it is essential to make the legal arrangements work at all institutions and departments. I follow this work closely in my dialogue with the health regions as part of the further development of the patient’s healthcare.

ParlaMint-NO_2018-12-05.ana.ud557e3255	In 1978, the Law on Self - Definitive Abortion was passed. There was a milestone in women's right to determine their own bodies and lives. Decision-making on abortion is not a simple decision, but the decision shall be owned and made by the woman. For the past few weeks we have been told that the government in negotiations with the Christian People's Party is speculating in removing the abortion law §2c and replacing it with a new paragraph. An argument put forward in the debate is that more attention should be paid to the situation of women. The abortion law, as we know it today, is neutrally designed and based on objective criteria. If the law is amended to give more emphasis to the woman’s situation, it will open up to a more discretion-based assessment. It leaves women in the denominator and explains why they are in a difficult situation. I don't want anyone to have to expand their life situation and argue why you're unfit to care for the child. Unfortunately, this is the consequence if the objective criteria are removed. In addition, it could lead to more spatial inequality. The Council of State itself has called for the reduction of the number of abortions, because the practice between the denominators is different and, in particular, the discretion-based paragraphs are interpreted differently. An unmarried poor woman would end up in a critical situation, at risk of serious injury, death, exclusion and years of condemnation. There were clearly social differences in the outcome it got for the woman. The main argument of the Right Party and the Christian People's Party to change the law is to remove what they describe as a ‘discriminatory element’. If more emphasis is placed on the woman's situation, the woman will be forced to stand in front of a denominator and proselyte why she should have an abortion. Many will experience it as an extra load – some as insurmountable. For eloquent women, or for women with good advice, there will always be a solution. An opportunity will be to travel to one of our neighbouring countries, but the wallet will decide. Early ultrasound in pregnancy is a prerequisite for finding out if the foetus needs any help. The smallest patients deserve the best health care available. And in those cases where not everything is going to be right, early ultrasound is an important opportunity to discover in time, discover early. Today, women over 38 years of age and other risk groups receive an offer of foetal diagnostics through public health services when they become pregnant. It involves early ultrasound and blood tests, so-called KUB testing. It is joyous and timely that the NIPT test is now coming into place, because it is a more precise and gentle test. NIPT prevents many morning or foetal water samples – which many people today have to take. They are risky and lead to spontaneous abortions every year. Pregnant in Norway deserves the best health service we can provide, the safest health service. Foetal diagnostics provide women with knowledge and facts that can be critical to the ability of the foetus to provide health care. It is the Council of State itself and the Prime Minister who has made Section 2 (c) the ‘Downs paragraph’. The use and expression originates exclusively from the last few weeks. The neutral paragraph embraces many different, serious diseases and conditions. The organisation Menschwerd has regretted that they referred to §2c as the Downs section. On questions from the Labour Party in the Question Time, the Prime Minister has been asked whether she believes that such a label, used for a neutral paragraph that embraces a lot of diseases, increases or decreases stigmatisation of people with Down’s syndrome. The Prime Minister chose not to be as clear in his regret as the organisation Human Rights. It's worth noting that the number of children born with Down syndrome has been stable in the last 15 years. It raises a number of questions: what is ‘viable’ and who is going to decide? Is a fetus viable when it can live for a few days, or a few weeks, with intensive treatment? Changing Section 2(c) to only a non-viable foetus would significantly reduce women’s right to abortion. Shall we ladies be forced to have to give birth to children so that they may die shortly afterwards? ParlaMint-NO_2018-12-05.ana.ud557e3285	As the Prime Minister and I have answered the Stortinget earlier on a number of oral and written questions: This government does not have a work going on to change today's abortion permit. This is not the only premise in the interpellant's question that is wrong. Since 1978, according to the abortion law, it has been the decision of the woman to commit abortion before the limit of self-determined abortion by the end of the 12th week. The law of abortion is based on an understanding that the legal position of the foetus is being increased along the way in pregnancy. When the self-determined abortion limit is set at 12 weeks, it is not a matter of the legislators at that time believing that the fetus does not have a self-value until the twelfth week, but that it was the woman who, before that time, was best suited to make that balance. After the 12th week, there is a shift in the protection of the foetus by society, by the decision to grant abortion being made by an abortion candidate according to politically defined criteria. Other parts of the interpellant’s questions are based on an article in the VG based on anonymous sources.
